Stackelberg Mean Field Games: convergence and existence results to the problem of Principal with multiple Agents in competition The author thanks Dylan Possamaï for his helpful suggestions and interesting discussions.
Mao Fabrice Djete

Abstract
In a situation of moral hazard, this paper investigates the problem of Principal with n n Agents when the number of Agents n n goes to infinity. There is competition between the Agents expressed by the fact that they optimize their utility functions through a Nash equilibrium criterion. Each Agent is offered by the Principal a contract which is divided into a Markovian part involving the state/production of the Agent and a non–Markovian part involving the states/productions of all the other Agents. The Agents are in interactions. These interactions are characterized by common noise, the empirical distribution of states/productions and controls, and the contract which is not assumed to be a map of the empirical distribution. By the help of the mean field games theory, we are able to formulate an appropriate l ​ i ​ m ​ i ​ t limit problem involving a Principal with a r ​ e ​ p ​ r ​ e ​ s ​ e ​ n ​ t ​ a ​ t ​ i ​ v ​ e representative Agent. We start by solving the problem of both the Principal and the r ​ e ​ p ​ r ​ e ​ s ​ e ​ n ​ t ​ a ​ t ​ i ​ v ​ e representative Agent in this l ​ i ​ m ​ i ​ t limit problem. Then, when n n goes to infinity, we show that the problem of Principal with n n Agents converges to the l ​ i ​ m ​ i ​ t limit problem of Principal with a r ​ e ​ p ​ r ​ e ​ s ​ e ​ n ​ t ​ a ​ t ​ i ​ v ​ e representative Agent. That is, first, any convergent sequence of (approximate) best responses from the n n Agents associated to an (approximate) n n –dependent optimal contract from the Principal in the problem of Principal with n n Agents converges to the best response of the r ​ e ​ p ​ r ​ e ​ s ​ e ​ n ​ t ​ a ​ t ​ i ​ v ​ e representative Agent in the l ​ i ​ m ​ i ​ t limit problem when the Principal offers the limit contract of the (approximate) n n –dependent optimal contract. The limit contract is also optimal for the Principal’s problem in the l ​ i ​ m ​ i ​ t limit problem. Second, given an optimal contract for the Principal and the associated best response from the r ​ e ​ p ​ r ​ e ​ s ​ e ​ n ​ t ​ a ​ t ​ i ​ v ​ e representative Agent, we are able to construct the (approximate) contract and the corresponding (approximate) best responses for the problem of Principal with n n Agents. A notable result is that, despite allowing a general type of contracts, it is approximately optimal for the Principal to offer contracts to the n n Agents that are maps of the empirical distribution of states/productions and controls of the Agents.



1
Introduction

By analyzing the relationship between a Principal and an Agent, the Principal–Agent theory turns out to play an important role in economics, management, game theory,
\cdots
This theory focuses on situations where one party, the Principal (employer, manager, or owner), delegates decision–making authority or tasks to another party, the Agent (employee, worker, or manager), who acts on the Principal’s behalf. The Principal–Agent relationship is prevalent in various settings, such as corporate governance, employment contracts, government administration, and more. By addressing the challenges that arise from the delegation of tasks and decision–making, this theory helps to design better contracts, governance structures, and incentive systems, leading to more effective and successful outcomes in various real–world settings.

The primary focus of the Principal–Agent theory is to design incentive mechanisms and contracts that align the interests of the Principal and the Agent, ensuring that the Agent acts in the best interest of the Principal. The central issue is how to design a contract that motivates the Agent to take actions that maximize the Principal’s welfare, even when the Agent’s own objectives may not align perfectly with those of the Principal. There is therefore an asymmetry of information which is known in the literature as
``
moral hazard
"
, as the Agent’s actions are not fully observable by the Principal. In the situation described, from a game theory perspective, this desire to find the appropriate contracts corresponding to the interests of the Principal and the Agent leads to the search for a Stackelberg equilibrium. Indeed, in simple terms, given a contract, the Principal will compute the potential best response of the Agent. Then, with this best response from the Agent, the Principal will try to maximize his utility and find the resulting optimal contract.

The problem of Principal–Agent has been extensively studied in the field of economics, and there is a significant body of literature that addresses its mathematical treatment.
Laffont and Martimort 2002
[
41
]
,
Schmitz 2006
[
54
]
,
Laffont and Tirole 1993
[
42
]
,
Salanié 1997
[
49
]
are some examples of classical references that provide some general overview on the topic while trying to solve some questions raised by the subject. The mathematical resolution has been treated in only static or discrete–time for a long time before the seminal paper of
Holmstrom and Milgrom 1987

[
30
]
who provided a way to deal with this problem in continuous time.
Schättler and Sung 1993

[
52
,
53
]
,
Sung 1995

[
55
]
,
Müller 1998
[
46
,
47
]
follow the basis of
[
30
]
and extend their work in various situations. By using techniques borrowed from the stochastic control theory (SCT), the notable work of
Sannikov 2008

[
50
,
51
]
brought some tools in the approach of the resolution of this problem. This use of SCT inspired
Cvitanić et al. 2018

[
11
,
10
]
to provide a general framework for solving the Princial–Agent problem. Their approach provides a generic way to deal with this problem that can be summarize as follows: given a contract, a resolution of Backward Stochastic Differential Equation (BSDE) helps solving the problem of the Agent. Then, the problem of the Principal is tackled by solving a classical stochastic control problem with two states variables: the output controlled by the Agent, and his continuation utility.

An important case from the point of view of applications is the consideration of several Agents in the presence of one Principal. It is sometimes referred to in the literature as the
``
multi–Agent Principal–Agent problem
"
. One Key challenge in the multi–Agent Principal–Agent problem is that the Principal needs to account for the interactions and dependencies between different Agents’ actions. The decisions of one Agent may affect the incentives or outcomes for other Agents, leading to potential coordination problems. Under various approaches/techniques, the multi–Agent Principal–Agent problem has been treated by several papers/books such as
Holmström 1982

[
29
]
,
Mookherjee 1984

[
45
]
,
Green and Stokey 1983

[
28
]
,
Demski and Sappington 1984

[
12
]
,
Koo et al. 2008

[
36
]
,
Elie and Possamaï 2019

[
22
]
,
\cdots
The Agents are generally assumed to be in competition, which is represented by the use of Nash equilibrium criteria for the maximization of their utility functions.
From the perspective of Stochastic Control Theory with the presence of competition between Agents, which is the approach we would like to emphasize here, it is worth emphasizing the paper of
[
22
]
(see also
Espinosa and Touzi 2015

[
25
]
) who uses the approach of
[
11
]
in the situation of mutlti–Agent. The resolution in terms of BSDE–(classical)Stochastic control Problem is still valid. However, among other things, the main difficulty is the handling of multi–dimensional BSDE. Contrary to the one dimensional setting, quadratic multi–dimensional BSDEs are known to suffer some ill–posedness see for instance
Frei and Reis 2011

[
27
]
.
Elie and Possamaï 2019

[
22
]
deal with this problem by imposing the wellposedness as a requirement for the admissibility of the contracts offered by the Principal. Since the optimal contracts they find at the end turn out to verify the admissibility they imposed, this restriction does not seem to be a huge limitation.

In many situations, the multi–Agent model is considered with a very large number of Agents (government policies, electricity management, crowd control,
\cdots
). It is well–known that as the number of Agents increases, the complexity of the problem also rises significantly especially in the presence of competitiveness among the Agents. The usual approach to treat this situation in the literature is to represent this large number of Agents through an appropriate unique entity. It is sometimes referred to as the problem of Principal–Agent with a continuum of Agents.
In the past recent years, when it comes down to deal with a large number/continuum of Agents in competition (Nash equilibria), it is becoming quite natural and appropriate to use Mean Field Games (MFG). Mean Field Games were introduced in the seminal works of
Lasry and Lions 2006

[
44
]
and
Huang et al. 2003

[
32
]
as a tool for the study of
n
–player game when the number of players
n
is very large. Since then it has been the subject of intensive study and use in many areas, see
Carmona and Delarue 2018

[
4
]
for an overview.

This approach by MFG to solve this problem has been used by
Elie et al. 2019

[
23
]
. In
[
23
]
, given a contract, the Principal computes the best response of the
representative
Agent by solving a MFG problem through some BSDEs. Here, the
representative
Agent can be seen as an entity characterizing all the Agents (the continuum of Agents). Then, with this best response, the Principal solves his problem. In their particular setting,
Elie et al. 2019

[
23
]
show that this Principal’s problem turns out to be a McKean–Vlasov stochastic control problem. Later, in order to analyze the electricity demand,
Elie et al. 2021

[
24
]
extend some aspects of
[
23
]
by adding a source of external noise impacting all the players called in the literature common–noise, and by considering the case where the diffusion coefficient can be controlled. In order to provide some ideas for epidemic control,
Aurell et al. 2022
[
2
]
have used this MFG(BSDE)–Mckean Vlasov stochastic control problem approach to treat the problem of Principal–Agent with a continuum of agents (see also
Carmona and Wang 2018
[
6
]
). This current paper puts itself in this problematic of Principal–Agent with multi–Agent/continuum of Agents.

Over the years, a lot of efforts have been made in the literature for solving the problem of Principal with
n
Agents (
n\geq 1
) and Principal with a continuum of Agents. These resolutions have been made under certain assumptions, either by providing explicit solutions or by showing existence results. With these resolutions in mind, some natural questions appear. In a certain sense, does the problem of Principal with
n
Agents converges to the problem of Principal with a continuum of Agents when
n\to\infty
? How much the assumptions usually considered can be weaken ? This paper tries to provide some answer regarding these questions among other things.

Although using quite different techniques, like
[
22
,
23
]
, in a situation of moral hazard, in this paper we address the problem of the Principal with
n
Agents / continuum of Agents using the framework and techniques of stochastic control theory. The Agents are considered to be in an environment of competitiveness, that is to say that they optimize their criteria through Nash equilibria. We begin by proposing a formulation of the problem of Principal with
n
Agents more general than the usual framework treated in the literature. We refer to
Section

2.1
for the proper mathematical description. Indeed, in the presence of a common noise, the output/production of the Agents is potentially impacted by the contract proposed by the Principal. This may seem like a small addition at first glance, but it has never been considered in the literature and generates technical problems. Given a contract, the Principal computes the best response of the Agents by looking for the Nash equilibria. Having this best response, the Principal determines what is the best contract to offer that maximizes his utility. We then formulate the associated limit problem of the Principal with a continuum of Agents. With an adequate notion of contracts (see
Section

2.2
for the proper definition), the problem turns out to be the following: given a contract, the Principal solves a specific MFG problem and obtains the best action of the representative Agent. After getting this best response, the Principal solves his problem which is a stochastic control of MFG solutions.

Under some general assumptions, we start by showing that this limit problem admit a solution i.e. there is an optimal contract maximizing the utility of the Principal associated to the (well–defined) best response of the representative Agent. Next, we prove that, with the optimal contract, we can construct an (approximate) optimal contract for the problem of Principal with
n
Agents with an associated (approximate) best response for the
n
Agents. Also, given any sequence of (approximate) optimal contracts and best responses (indexes by
n
) for the problem of Principal with
n
Agents, putting in an appropriate space, we are able to show that the sequence of optimal contracts and best responses are relatively compact. In addition, any limit contract is optimal for the limit problem formulated and any limit best response is the best response of the representative Agent. All these results allow us to provide the convergence of the problem of the Principal with
n
Agents to the problem of the Principal with a continuum of Agents when
n\to\infty
.

If we put aside the proof given in
[
23
, Theorem 5.3.]
in a very specific case/example, this paper seems to be the first to give a rigorous proof of the connection between the problem of the Principal with
n
Agents and the problem of the Principal with a continuum of Agents when
n\to\infty
. Although we do not assume any specific dependence in the contracts, a notable fact is that our result leads to see that it is (approximately) optimal to consider contracts depending on the empirical distribution of the agents’ output in the problem of Principal with
n
Agents. Also, the assumptions we consider here seem to be more general than what is usually considered in the literature. Especially, our existence result in the limit problem is more general than the one mentioned in the literature. Our approach/technique turns out to be quite different from what is done in the literature of Principal–Agent. Namely, contrary to the use of BSDEs as done in
[
23
]
, although presented differently, our proof is inspired by the notion of relaxed solutions in stochastic control problem initiated by
El Karoui et al. 1987
[
21
]
(see
Lacker 2016
[
37
]
and
Djete et al. 0

[
17
]
for the MFG and Mckean–Vlasov control problem). It is worth mentioning that the framework and the techniques used here share some similitude to
Djete 2023a

[
15
]
.
Despite the general aspects of our framework, our results suffer the fact that, given an Agent
i(\leq n)
, the Principal needs to only offer contract that is separated in one non–Markovian part which represents the impact of the production of all the other Agents in the contract and one Markovian part which is the impact of the production of the Agent
i
. Another limitation is the inability to consider a control in the diffusion coefficients. Despite this limitation, this paper seems to provide results that provide answers to some natural questions arising in the Principal–Agent problem not addressed so far.

The paper is organised as follows. After briefly recalling some notations,
Section

2
describes the proper setting considered as well as the main results of this article. Namely,
Section

2.1
formulates the problem of the Principal with
n
Agents. Then, in
Section

2.2
, the associated limit formulation i.e. the problem of the Principal with a representative Agent is given.
Section

2.3
is finally dedicated to the statement of the main results. All proofs are provided in
Section

3
.

Notations
.

(i)

Given a
Polish
space
(E,\Delta)
and
p\geq 1,
we denote by
{\cal P}(E)
the collection of all Borel probability measures on
E
,
and by
{\cal P}_{p}(E)
the subset of Borel probability measures
\mu

such that
\int_{E}\Delta(e,e_{0})^{p}\mu(de)<\infty
for some
e_{0}\in E
.
We equip
{\cal P}_{p}(E)
with the Wasserstein metric
{\cal W}_{p}
defined by

{\cal W}_{p}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})~:=~\bigg(\inf_{\lambda\in\Lambda(\mu,\mu^{\prime})}\int_{E\times E}\Delta(e,e^{\prime})^{p}~\lambda(\mathrm{d}e,\mathrm{d}e^{\prime})\bigg)^{1/p},

where
\Lambda(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
denotes the collection of all probability measures
\lambda
on
E\times E

such that
\lambda(\mathrm{d}e,E)=\mu
and
\lambda(E,\mathrm{d}e^{\prime})=\mu^{\prime}(\mathrm{d}e^{\prime})
. Equipped with
{\cal W}_{p},

{\cal P}_{p}(E)
is a Polish space (see
[
56
, Theorem 6.18]
). For any
\mu\in{\cal P}(E)
and
\mu
–integrable function
\varphi:E\to\mathbb{R},
we define

\displaystyle\langle\varphi,\mu\rangle=\langle\mu,\varphi\rangle:=\int_{E}\varphi(e)\mu(\mathrm{d}e),

and for another metric space
(E^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
, we denote by
\mu\otimes\mu^{\prime}\in{\cal P}(E\times E^{\prime})
the product probability of any
(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\in{\cal P}(E)\times{\cal P}(E^{\prime})
.

(ii)
Let
(S,\Delta)
and
(S^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
be two Polish space with the Borel
\sigma
–fields
{\cal F}
and
{\cal F}^{\prime}
respectively. An application
V:S\to S^{\prime}
will be called universally measurable if
V
is a measurable map from
(S,{\cal F}^{U})
to
(S^{\prime},{\cal F}^{\prime})
where
{\cal F}^{U}
is the universal completion of
{\cal F}
i.e.
{\cal F}^{U}:=\cap_{\mathbb{P}\in{\cal P}(S)}{\cal F}^{\mathbb{P}}
where
{\cal F}^{\mathbb{P}}
is the
\mathbb{P}
–completed
\sigma
–field of
{\cal F}
.
Given a probability space
(\Omega,{\cal H},\mathbb{P})
supporting a sub-
\sigma
-algebra
{\cal G}\subset{\cal H}
then for a Polish space
E
and any random variable
\xi:\Omega\longrightarrow E
, both the notations
{\cal L}^{\mathbb{P}}(\xi|{\cal G})(\omega)
and
\mathbb{P}^{{\cal G}}_{\omega}\circ(\xi)^{-1}
are used to denote the conditional distribution of
\xi
knowing
{\cal G}
under
\mathbb{P}
.

(iii)
For any
(E,\Delta)
and
(E^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
two Polish spaces, we use
C_{b}(E,E^{\prime})
to denote the set of continuous functions
f
from
E
into
E^{\prime}
such that
\sup_{e\in E}\Delta^{\prime}(f(e),e^{\prime}_{0})<\infty
for some
e^{\prime}_{0}\in E^{\prime}
.
Let
\mathbb{N}^{*}
denote the set of positive integers. Given non–negative integers
m
and
n
, we denote by
\mathbb{S}^{m\times n}
the collection of all
m\times n
–dimensional matrices with real entries, equipped with the standard Euclidean norm, which we denote by
|\cdot|
regardless of the dimensions.
We also denote
\mathbb{S}^{n}:=\mathbb{S}^{n\times n}
, and denote by
0_{m\times n}
the element in
\mathbb{S}^{m\times n}
whose entries are all
0
, and by
\mathrm{I}_{n}
the identity matrix in
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

(iv)

Let
T>0
and
(\Sigma,\rho)
be a Polish space, we denote by
C([0,T];\Sigma)
the space of all continuous functions on
[0,T]
taking values in
\Sigma
.
When
\Sigma=\mathbb{R}^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we simply write
{\cal C}^{k}:=C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{k}),
also we shall denote by
{\cal C}^{k}_{{\cal W}}:=C([0,T];{\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^{k})).

With a Polish space
E
, we denote by
\mathbb{M}(E)
the space of all Borel measures
q(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}e)
on
[0,T]\times E
,
whose marginal distribution on
[0,T]
is the Lebesgue measure
\mathrm{d}t
,
that is to say
q(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}e)=q(t,\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}t
for a family
(q(t,\mathrm{d}e))_{t\in[0,T]}
of Borel probability measures on
E
.
For any
q\in\mathbb{M}(E)
and
t\in[0,T],
we define
q_{t\wedge\cdot}\in\mathbb{M}(E)
by

q_{t\wedge\cdot}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}e):=q(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}e)\big|_{[0,t]\times E}+\delta_{e_{0}}(\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}s\big|_{(t,T]\times E},\;\text{for some fixed $e_{0}\in E$.}

(1.1)

We will say that
q\in\mathbb{M}_{0}(E)
if
q\in\mathbb{M}(E)
and there exists a Borel map
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\overline{e}(t)\in E
such that
q=\delta_{\overline{e}(t)}(\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}t
.
A map
h:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times C([0,T];\Sigma)\times\mathbb{M}(E)
is called a progressively Borel measurable if it verifies
h(t,x,\pi,q)=h(t,x,\pi_{t\wedge\cdot},q_{t\wedge\cdot}),
for any
(t,x,\pi,q)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times C([0,T];\Sigma)\times\mathbb{M}(E).

2
Setup and main results

The general assumptions used throughout this paper are now formulated. The dimension
d\geq 1
, the nonempty Polish spaces
(A,\rho_{A})
,
({\rm E},\rho_{\rm E})
and
({\rm I},\rho_{\rm I})
, and the
time horizon

T>0
are fixed. We shall denote
{\cal P}_{A}
for the space of all Borel probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A
, i.e.
{\cal P}_{A}:={\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A).
Also, we set
p\geq 2,

\nu\in{\cal P}_{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
p^{\prime}>p,
and the probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{H}:=({\cal H}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]},{\cal H},\mathbb{P})
1
1

1

The probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{H},\mathbb{P})
contains as many random variables as we want in the sense that: each time we need a sequence of independent uniform random variables or Brownian motions, we can find them on
\Omega
with the distribution
\mathbb{P}
without mentioning an enlarging of the space.
. We are given the following progressively Borel measurable functions:

\left(b,L\right):[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{\cal C}_{{\cal W}}\times A\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\;\;\left(\overline{b},\overline{L},\overline{L}_{\mathrm{P}}\right):[0,T]\times{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\sigma:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{S}^{d},

and the Borel maps
U:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\Upsilon:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
(g,g_{\mathrm{P}}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{\rm I}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
and
(\overline{g},\overline{g}_{\mathrm{P}}):C_{{\cal W}}\times{\rm E}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}

Assumption 2.1
.

(i)

A
,
{\rm E}
and
{\rm I}
are nonempty convex compact
Polish sets
;

(ii)
The map
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{\cal C}_{{\cal W}}\times A\times{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}\ni(t,x,\pi,a,m,e)\mapsto\left(b(t,x,\pi,a),\overline{b}(t,m,e),\sigma(t,x)\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d}
is bounded Lipschitz in
(x,\pi,m)
uniformly in
(t,a,e)
, and continuous in
(x,\pi,a,m,e)
for any
t
;

(iii)

Non–degeneracy condition:

\inf_{(t,x)}\sigma\sigma^{\top}(t,x)>0
and
\sigma_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d}
is an invertible matrix;

(iv)
The map
(t,x,\pi,a,m,e,i,v,u)\mapsto\left(L(t,x,\pi,a),(\overline{L},\overline{L}_{\mathrm{P}})(t,m,e),(g,g_{\mathrm{P}})(x,i),(\overline{g},\overline{g}_{\mathrm{P}})(\pi,e),\Upsilon(v),U(u)\right)
is continuous in
(x,\pi,a,m,e,i,v,u)
for each
t
, and with
p
–linear growth in
(x,\pi,m,v,u)
uniformly in
(t,a,e,i)
.

Remark 2.2
.

The maps
(b,L,g,\overline{b},\overline{L},\overline{g},\sigma)
are related to the Agents and the maps
(g_{\mathrm{P}},\overline{L}_{\mathrm{P}},\overline{g}_{\mathrm{P}},\Upsilon,U)
will be associated to the Principal. Some of the conditions can be relaxed especially the fact that the maps
(b,\overline{b},\sigma)
are bounded and the sets
(A,{\rm E},{\rm I})
compact. We have chosen these conditions to allow the reader to concentrate on the main points instead of being confused by long classical technicalities generated by these considerations.

2.1
Principal and
n
Agents

Let
n\geq 1
. Before introducing the proper mathematical framework we will consider here. Let us first defining what we call controls and contracts in the situation of the problem of Principal with
n
Agents.

\boldsymbol{\rm Controls}
: We denote by
{\cal A}_{n}
the set of all progressively Borel measurable maps
\alpha:[0,T]\times({\cal C}^{d})^{n}\to A
. Any element
n
–tuple
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha^{1},\cdots,\alpha^{n})\in({\cal A}_{n})^{n}
belongs to
{\cal D}_{n}
if there exists a progressively Borel measurable map
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{\cal C}_{{\cal W}}\ni(t,x,\pi)\mapsto\widehat{\alpha}(t,x,\pi)\in A
Lipschitz in
(x,\pi)
uniformly in
t
s.t.
\alpha^{i}(t,\boldsymbol{x})=\widehat{\alpha}(t,x^{i}(t),\pi^{n})
for each
t\in[0,T]
and
1\leq i\leq n
where
\boldsymbol{x}=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})\in({\cal C}^{d})^{n}
and
\pi^{n}(t):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x^{i}(t)}
.

\boldsymbol{\rm Contracts}
: We will say
\mathfrak{C}^{n}:=(\phi^{n},\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})
is a contract if:
\phi^{n}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to{\rm I}
and
\xi^{n}:({\cal C}^{d})^{n}\to{\rm E}
are Borel maps, and
\aleph^{n}:[0,T]\times({\cal C}^{d})^{n}\to{\rm E}
is a progressively Borel measurable map. A contract
\mathfrak{C}^{n}:=(\phi^{n},\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})
will be called
{distributed}
contract if there exists a Borel map
[0,T]\times{\cal C}_{{\cal W}}\ni(t,\pi)\mapsto\left(\widehat{\aleph}^{n}(t,\pi),\widehat{\xi}^{n}(\pi)\right)\in{\rm E}\times{\rm E}
Lipschitz in
\pi
uniformly in
t
s.t.
\aleph^{n}(t,\boldsymbol{x})=\widehat{\aleph}^{n}(t,\pi^{n})
and
\xi^{n}(\boldsymbol{x})=\widehat{\xi}^{n}(\pi^{n})
where
\boldsymbol{x}=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})\in({\cal C}^{d})^{n}
and
\pi^{n}(t):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x^{i}(t)}
.

Remark 2.3
.

A contract
\mathfrak{C}^{n}=(\phi^{n},\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})
offered by the Principal consists, on the one hand, of
(\phi^{n},\xi^{n})
which represents the final payments received at time
T
by the Agents. And, on the other hand, of
\aleph^{n}
which is the instantaneous payments given to the Agents at time
t\in[0,T]
.

2.1.1
The problem of the
n
Agents

On the filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{H},{\cal H},\mathbb{P}),
let
(W^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
be a sequence of independent
\mathbb{R}^{d}
–valued
\mathbb{H}
–adapted Brownian motions,
B
be an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
–valued
\mathbb{H}
–adapted Brownian motion and
(\iota^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
a sequence of iid
{\cal H}_{0}
–random variables of law
\nu.
Besides,
(W^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{*}},

B
and
(\iota^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
are independent. Then, given a contract
{\mathfrak{C}}^{n}:=(\phi^{n},\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})
and the controls
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}:=(\alpha^{1,n},\dots,\alpha^{n,n})\in({\cal A}_{n})^{n}
, we denote by
\mathbf{X}^{\aleph^{n},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}}:=\mathbf{X}=(X^{1}_{\cdot},\dots,X^{n}_{\cdot})
the process satisfying: for each
i\in\{1,\dots,n\},

X^{i}_{0}:=\iota^{i}
,
\mathbb{E}\big[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|X^{i}_{t}|^{p}\big]<\infty,
and
\mathbb{P}
–a.e.

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X^{i}_{t}=\overline{b}\left(t,\overline{\varphi}^{n}_{t}[\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}],\aleph^{n}(t,\mathbf{X})\right)+b\left(t,X^{i}_{t},\varphi^{n}[\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}],\alpha^{i,n}(t,\mathbf{X})\right)\;\;\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,X^{i}_{t})\mathrm{d}W^{i}_{t}+\sigma_{0}\mathrm{d}B_{t}

(2.1)

with

\varphi^{n}_{t}[\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}](\mathrm{d}x):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X^{i}_{t}}(\mathrm{d}x)\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\overline{\varphi}^{n}_{t}[\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}](\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}a):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\big(X^{i}_{t},\;\alpha^{i,n}(t,\mathbf{X})\big)}(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}a),~\mbox{for all}~~t\in[0,T].

The reward value of player
i
associated with the contract
\mathfrak{C}^{n}=(\phi^{n},\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})
and the control rule/strategy
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}:=(\alpha^{1,n},\dots,\alpha^{n,n})
is then defined by

\displaystyle J^{\mathfrak{C}^{n}}_{n,i}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\overline{L}\left(t,\overline{\varphi}^{n}_{t}[\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}],\aleph^{n}(t,\mathbf{X})\right)+L\left(t,X^{i}_{t},{\varphi}^{n}[\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}],\alpha^{i,n}(t,\mathbf{X})\right)\;\mathrm{d}t+\overline{g}\left({\varphi}^{n}[\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}],\xi^{n}(\mathbf{X})\right)+{g}\left(X^{i}_{T},\phi^{n}(X^{i}_{T})\right)\right].

Remark 2.4
.

(i)
By using Girsanov’s Theorem, we can see that
Equation

2.1
is uniquely defined in distribution. However, since we only consider that the maps
(\alpha^{1,n},\cdots,\alpha^{n,n})
and
\aleph^{n}
are Borel measurable, the strong well–posedness of
Equation

2.1
is unclear. The process
\mathbf{X}
should therefore be seen as a weak solution. We may need to extend
(\Omega,\mathbb{H},\mathbb{P})
to write
\mathbf{X}
, but to avoid heavy notations, we assumed that
(\Omega,\mathbb{H},\mathbb{P})
is such that we do not need any further extensions
(
see
Footnote

1
)
.

(ii)
The processes
\mathbf{X}
represent the production of all the
n
Agents and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}
their associated controls.
In the final payment
(\phi^{n},\xi^{n})
, given an Agent
i
,
\phi^{n}
is the Markovian part involving the terminal production of the Agent i.e.
X^{i}_{T}
and
\xi^{n}
is the non–Markovian part involving the production of all the Agents i.e.
\mathbf{X}
. However, the instantaneous payment
\aleph^{n}
is only non–Markovian and is composed of the production of all the Agents.

(iii)
It is worth mentioning that, even without the presence of the empirical distribution
\overline{\varphi}^{n}[\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}]
, the processes
(X^{1},\cdots,X^{n})
are in interactions because of the presence of the common noise and especially of
\aleph^{n}
. Besides,
\aleph^{n}
is not necessary a map of the empirical distribution
\overline{\varphi}^{n}[\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}]
, but it just depends on
(X^{1},\cdots,X^{n})
.

We now give the optimization criterion considered between the Agents, that is to say what we will call approximate Nash equilibria.

Definition 2.5
.

((approximate) equilibria)

Let
n\geq 1
,
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+},
and the contract
\mathfrak{C}^{n}:=(\phi^{n},\aleph^{n},\xi^{n})
. We will say that a control rule/strategy
(\alpha^{1,n},\dots,\alpha^{n,n})\in({\cal A}_{n})^{n}
is an
\varepsilon
–
Nash equilibrium
if

J^{\mathfrak{C}^{n}}_{n,i}(\alpha^{1,n},\dots,\alpha^{n,n})\geq\sup_{\beta\in{\cal A}_{n}}J^{\mathfrak{C}^{n}}_{n,i}\big(\alpha^{1,n},\dots,\alpha^{i-1,n},\beta,\alpha^{i+1,n},\dots,\alpha^{n,n}\big)-\varepsilon,\;\mbox{for each}\;i\in\{1,\dots,n\}.

We set

\displaystyle\mbox{\rm NE}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon]:=\left\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}=(\alpha^{1,n},\cdots,\alpha^{n,n})\mbox{ an $\varepsilon$--Nash equilibrium given }\mathfrak{C}^{n}=(\phi^{n},\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})\right\}.

In addition, we denote by
\mbox{\rm NE}^{\rm dist}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon]
the subset of
\mbox{\rm NE}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon]
containing all the
n
–tuple
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}
belonging to
{\cal D}_{n}
.

Remark 2.6
.

If the contract
(\phi^{n},\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})
is a
distributed
contract independent of
n
, by the help of the
MFG
theory, the set
{\rm NE}^{\rm dist}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon]
turns out to be non–empty
(
see for instance
[
15
, Theorem 2.12.]
)
. However, as the map
(\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})
is only a Borel measurable map of
n
variables, it is worth mentioning that the set
{\rm NE}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon]
can be empty.

2.1.2
The Principal’s problem

In case
{\rm NE}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon]
is non–empty, most of the time
{\rm NE}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon]
is not a singleton, it contains many solutions. In this situation, the choice of the controls of the Agents is given to the Principal as it is done in the literature (see for instance
[
22
,
23
]
). The interpretation is that, first, the Principal must know whether the Agents will agree to the proposed contract and anticipate the response/behaviour of the Agents. Second, once the contract is offered, the Principal also recommends a behavior to the Agents. However, there is a minimum level of utility below which Agents will not consider the contract, it is called the reservation utility and denoted here
R_{0}-\varepsilon_{0}.
Keeping this mechanism in mind, we then formulate the problem of the Principal.

We fix
R_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}\geq 0
, we define
\underline{\rm NE}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
the set of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\rm NE}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon]
s.t.
J^{\mathfrak{C}^{n}}_{n,i}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\geq R_{0}-\varepsilon_{0}
for each
1\leq i\leq n
. In a same way we define
\underline{\rm NE}^{\rm dist}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
.
We now denote by
\Xi_{n}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
the set of admissible contracts
\mathfrak{C}^{n}=(\phi^{n},\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})
verifying:
\underline{\rm NE}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
is non–empty. Besides,
\mathfrak{C}^{n}=(\phi^{n},\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})\in\Xi_{n}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
will belong to
\Xi_{n}^{\rm dist}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
if
\mathfrak{C}^{n}
is a
distributed
contract and
\underline{\rm NE}^{\rm dist}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
is non–empty.

Let us consider the two problems faced by the Principal

\displaystyle V_{n,\mathrm{P}}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]:=\sup_{\mathfrak{C}^{n}\in\Xi_{n}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]}\;\;\sup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}\in\underline{\rm NE}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]}J_{n,\mathrm{P}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}}}(\mathfrak{C}^{n})\;\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\;V^{\rm dist}_{n,\mathrm{P}}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]:=\sup_{\mathfrak{C}^{n}\in\Xi^{\rm dist}_{n}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]}\;\;\sup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}\in\underline{\rm NE}^{\rm dist}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]}J_{n,\mathrm{P}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}}}(\mathfrak{C}^{n})

where the reward of the Principal is

\displaystyle J_{n,\mathrm{P}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}}}(\mathfrak{C}^{n}):=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[U\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Upsilon\left(X^{i}_{T}\right)-g_{\mathrm{P}}\left(X^{i}_{T},\phi^{n}(X^{i}_{T})\right)-\overline{g}_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\varphi^{n}[\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}],\xi^{n}(\mathbf{X})\right)-\int_{0}^{T}\overline{L}_{\mathrm{P}}\left(t,\overline{\varphi}^{n}_{t}[\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}],\aleph^{n}(t,\mathbf{X})\right)\;\mathrm{d}t\right)\right].

Remark 2.7
.

The actual reservation utility is
R_{0}
. Nevertheless, we consider that the Agents are willing to go a little below i.e.
R_{0}-\varepsilon_{0}
. The interpretation is that the Principal is in a strong position to negotiate and compromise with the finite number of Agents. However, when the number of Agents is growing, the Agents are less and less willing to budge from their positions as they are feeling strong, therefore
\varepsilon_{0}
becomes more and more small. In the end, in the case of an infinite number of Agents that we will see below, they will no longer be willing to go below
R_{0}
.

2.2
Limit problem : Stochastic Control of Mean Field Games Solutions

For convenience, we are still on the probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{H},\mathbb{P})
. On this space, we consider
(W,B)
an
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
–valued
\mathbb{H}
–Brownian motion and an
{\cal H}_{0}
–random variable
\iota
s.t.
{\cal L}(\iota)=\nu
.

We set
\mathbb{M}:=\mathbb{M}({\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E})
and
\mathbb{M}_{0}:=\mathbb{M}_{0}({\cal P}_{A}\times\mathbb{E})
. For any progressively Borel measurable map
h:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{\cal C}_{\cal W}\times\mathbb{M}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
\left(t,x,\pi,q=\delta_{\overline{e}(t)}(\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}t\right)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{\cal C}_{\cal W}\times\mathbb{M}_{0}
, for notational convenience, we will write
h(t,x,\pi,\overline{e})
instead of
h(t,x,\pi,q)
. Also, whenever we write
h(t,x,\pi,\overline{e})
where
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\overline{e}(t)\in{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}
is a Borel map, it should be understood as
h\left(t,x,\pi,\delta_{\overline{e}(t)}(\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}t\right)
.

\boldsymbol{\rm Controls}
: In the case of the limit problem, we denote by
{\cal A}
the set of all progressively Borel measurable maps
\alpha:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}_{0}\to A
.

\boldsymbol{\rm Contracts}
: We will say
\mathfrak{C}:=(\phi,\xi,\aleph)
is a contract if:
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to{\rm I}
and
\xi:\mathbb{M}_{0}\to{\rm E}
are Borel maps, and
\aleph=(\aleph_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is an
{\rm E}
–valued
\mathbb{H}
–predictable process. Besides,
(\aleph,B)
,
\iota
and
W
are independent.

Remark 2.8
.

To make the connection with the framework of the Principal and
n
Agents, here for a contract
\mathfrak{C}=(\phi,\xi,\aleph)
,
(\phi,\xi)
represents the final payment and
\aleph
is the instantaneous fees.

2.2.1
The representative Agent problem

Given
\varepsilon\geq 0
and a contract
\mathfrak{C}:=(\phi,\xi,\aleph)
, we introduce the set of (approximate) MFG solutions
\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon]
. We say
(\alpha,\overline{\mu})
belongs to
\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon]
if:
\alpha\in{\cal A}
,
\overline{\mu}=(\overline{\mu}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is an
{\cal P}_{A}
–valued
\mathbb{H}
–predictable process,

\displaystyle J^{\mathfrak{C}}_{A,\overline{\mu}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha})\geq J^{\mathfrak{C}}_{A,\overline{\mu}}(\beta)-\varepsilon,\;\;\mbox{for any }\beta\in{\cal A}

with the reward
J^{\mathfrak{C}}_{A,\overline{\mu}}
defined by

\displaystyle J_{A,\overline{\mu}}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\beta):=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\overline{L}\left(t,\overline{\mu}_{t},\aleph_{t}\right)+L\left(t,X^{\beta}_{t},\mu_{t},\beta(t,X^{\beta}_{t},\overline{\mu},\aleph)\right)\mathrm{d}t+\overline{g}\left(\mu_{T},\xi(\overline{\mu},\aleph)\right)+g\left(X^{\beta}_{T},\phi(X^{\beta}_{T})\right)\bigg],

and
\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|X^{\beta}_{t}|^{p}\right]<\infty
,
X^{\beta}_{0}:=\iota
,
\mathbb{P}
–a.e.

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X^{\beta}_{t}=\overline{b}\left(t,\overline{\mu}_{t},\aleph_{t}\right)+b\left(t,X^{\beta}_{t},\mu_{t},\beta(t,X^{\beta}_{t},\overline{\mu},\aleph)\right)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,X^{\beta}_{t})\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\sigma_{0}\mathrm{d}B_{t},\;\;\;\mu_{t}=\overline{\mu}_{t}(\mathrm{d}x,A).

In addition, for
R:=\delta_{\left(\overline{\mu}_{t},\aleph_{t}\right)}(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}t
, the variables
\left(B,R\right)
,
W
and
X_{0}
are independent, the filtration
\mathbb{G}
is defined by
\mathbb{G}=\left({\cal G}_{t}:=\sigma\{R_{t\wedge\cdot},B_{t\wedge\cdot}\}\right)_{t\in[0,T]}

with,
\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\otimes\mathrm{d}t
–a.e.

\displaystyle{\cal L}(X^{\alpha}_{t}|{\cal G}_{T})=\mu_{t},\;\;\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}\left[L\left(t,X^{\alpha}_{t},{\cal L}\left(X^{\alpha}_{t}|{\cal G}_{t}\right),\alpha(t,X^{\alpha}_{t},\overline{\mu},\aleph)\right)|{\cal G}_{T}\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A}L\left(x,\mu_{t},a\right)\overline{\mu}_{t}(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}a)

(2.2)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}\left[\varphi(X^{\alpha}_{t})b\left(t,X^{\alpha}_{t},{\cal L}\left(X^{\alpha}_{t}|{\cal G}_{t}\right),\alpha(t,X^{\alpha}_{t},\overline{\mu},\aleph)\right)|{\cal G}_{T}\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A}\varphi(x)b\left(x,\mu_{t},a\right)\overline{\mu}_{t}(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}a)\;\;\mbox{for all $\varphi\in C_{c}$.}

(2.3)

When
\varepsilon=0,
we will simply write
\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C}]
instead of
\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C},0]
. The elements of
\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon]
are in the spirit of
weak
solutions of MFG in closed–loop setting (see
Lacker 2020
[
38
]
,
Lacker and Flem 2023
[
39
]
,
[
15
]
). We say that the map
(\alpha,\xi,\aleph)
belong to
{\cal C}_{s}
if
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times{\cal C}_{{\cal W}}\ni(t,x,\pi)\mapsto\left(\alpha(t,x,\pi),\aleph(t,\pi),\xi(\pi)\right)\in A\times{\rm E}\times{\rm E}
is Lipschitz in
(x,\pi)
uniformly in
t
. We use this definition to introduce the set of (approximate)
strong solution

\mbox{{\rm MFG}}_{S}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon]
by

\displaystyle\mbox{{\rm MFG}}_{S}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon]:=\Big\{(\alpha,\overline{\mu})\in\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon]:\;\exists\;(\widehat{\alpha},\widehat{\xi},\widehat{\aleph})\in{\cal C}_{s}\;\mbox{s.t.}\;

\displaystyle\overline{\mu}_{t}={\cal L}\left(X^{\alpha}_{t},\widehat{\alpha}(t,X^{\alpha}_{t},\mu)|B_{t\wedge\cdot}\right),

\displaystyle\aleph_{t}=\widehat{\aleph}(t,\mu),\;\xi(\overline{\mu},\aleph)=\widehat{\xi}(\mu)\;\;\;\mbox{
a.e. }(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times[0,T]\Big\}.

Remark 2.9
.

(i)
Notice that, for
(\alpha,\overline{\mu})\in\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon]
, the
(
approximate
)
optimal control
\alpha
does not necessary verify the classical consistency condition of the
MFG
of controls literature i.e.
\overline{\mu}_{t}={\cal L}(X^{\alpha}_{t},\alpha(t,X^{\alpha}_{t},\overline{\mu},\aleph)|{\cal G}_{T})

(
see
Carmona and Lacker 2015
[
5
]
,
Kobeissi 2022

[
35
]
,
[
15
]

)
. We only need to check that
\alpha
satisfies
Equation

2.2
and
Equation

2.3
which is associated to the maps
b
and
L
.

(ii)
For any contract
\mathfrak{C}
, under some conditions, we will see that
\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C}]
is non–empty. Besides, for
[0,T]\times{\cal C}_{{\cal W}}\ni(t,\pi)\mapsto\left(\overline{e}(t,\pi),z(\pi)\right)\in{\rm E}\times{\rm E}
a Lipschitz map in
\pi
uniformly in
t
, there is
(\alpha,\overline{\mu})\in\mbox{{\rm MFG}}_{S}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon]
for each
\varepsilon>0
where
\mathfrak{C}=(\phi,\xi,\aleph)
with
\aleph_{t}=\overline{e}(t,\mu)
and
\xi(\overline{\mu},\aleph)=z(\mu)
.

2.2.2
The Principal’s problem

For any
\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}\geq 0
, with the reservation utility
R_{0}
, like in
Section

2.1.2
, we introduce
\underline{\rm MFG}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
the set of
(\alpha,\overline{\mu})\in\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon]
s.t.
J^{\mathfrak{C}}_{A,\overline{\mu}}(\alpha)\geq R_{0}-\varepsilon_{0}
. Similarly we define
\underline{\rm MFG}_{S}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
. We now consider
\Xi[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
the set of admissible contracts
\mathfrak{C}=(\phi,\xi,\aleph)
i.e.
\underline{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
is non–empty. Also,
\Xi_{S}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
will denote the subset of
\mathfrak{C}\in\Xi[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
s.t.
\underline{\rm MFG}_{S}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
is non–empty. When
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{0}=0
, we simply write
\Xi
,
\underline{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C}]
,
\Xi_{S}
and
\underline{{\rm MFG}}_{S}[\mathfrak{C}]
. Let us consider the following problems

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{P}}:=\sup_{\mathfrak{C}\in\Xi}\;\;\sup_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\alpha,\overline{\mu})}\in\underline{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C}]}J_{\mathrm{P}}^{\alpha,\overline{\mu}}(\mathfrak{C})~~~~\mbox{and}~~~~V_{\mathrm{P}}^{S}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]:=\sup_{\mathfrak{C}\in\Xi_{S}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]}\;\;\sup_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\alpha,\overline{\mu})}\in\underline{{\rm MFG}}_{S}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon]}J_{\mathrm{P}}^{\alpha,\overline{\mu}}(\mathfrak{C})

(2.4)

where

\displaystyle J_{\mathrm{P}}^{\alpha,\overline{\mu}}(\mathfrak{C}):=\mathbb{E}\left[U\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\Upsilon\left(X^{\alpha}_{T}\right)-g_{\mathrm{P}}\left(X^{\alpha}_{T},\phi(X^{\alpha}_{T})\right)-\overline{g}_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\mu,\xi(\overline{\mu},\aleph)\right)-\int_{0}^{T}\overline{L}_{\mathrm{P}}\left(t,\overline{\mu}_{t},\aleph_{t}\right)\;\mathrm{d}t\Big|{\cal G}_{T}\right]\right)\right].

Remark 2.10
.

To follow up on the discussion started in
Remark

2.7
, in
V_{\mathrm{P}}
the representative Agent, representing an infinite number of Agents, is considering a utility greater than or equal to
R_{0}
and will not agree to drop below this level. However, in
V^{S}_{\mathrm{P}}
, the representative Agent will be willing to compromise by accepting
R_{0}-\varepsilon_{0}
. The idea is that the representative Agent agrees to go below
R_{0}
if he obtains contracts with a simpler structure than in
V_{\mathrm{P}}
, this is the case in
V^{S}_{\mathrm{P}}
.

2.3
Main limit results

In this section, we provide the main results of this paper. We start by an existence result for the problem of the Principal with a representative Agent. For this purpose, let us consider additional assumptions. Since
{\cal P}(A)
is compact, we say
m\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};{\cal P}(A))
if
m:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to{\cal P}(A)
is just a Borel measurable map. For any
\eta\in{\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
m\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};{\cal P}(A))
, we denote by
[\eta,m]\in{\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A)
defined by
[\eta,m]:=m(x)(\mathrm{d}a)\eta(\mathrm{d}x)
. Also, for
\pi\in{\cal C}_{{\cal W}}
, we set
{\rm K}_{b,\pi,m}(x):=\int_{A}b(t,x,\pi,a)m(x)(\mathrm{d}a)
for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Notice that
K_{b,\pi,m}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded Borel map i.e.
K_{b,\pi,m}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Assumption 2.11
.

The utility map of the Principal
U:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is non–decreasing and concave. For any
(t,x,\pi)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{\cal C}_{{\cal W}}
,
\left\{\left(b(t,x,\pi,a),\;z\right):\;\;z\leq L(t,x,\pi,a),\;a\in A\right\}
and

\displaystyle\bigg\{\Big(K_{b,\pi,m},\;\left(\overline{b},\overline{L}\right)\left(t,[\pi(t),m],e\right),\;z,\;\overline{z}_{\mathrm{P}}\Big):\;

\displaystyle m\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d};{\cal P}(A)\right)\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;e\in{\rm E}\;\;\mbox{with}\;\;

\displaystyle z\leq\langle L(t,\cdot,\pi,\cdot),[\pi(t),m]\rangle,\;\;\;\overline{z}_{\mathrm{P}}\leq-\overline{L}_{\mathrm{P}}\left(t,[\pi(t),m],e\right)\bigg\}

are closed convex sets. Furthermore, for each
(\pi,x)\in{\cal C}_{{\cal W}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\left\{\left(\overline{g}(\pi,e),\;\overline{z}_{\mathrm{P}}\right):\;\;\overline{z}_{\mathrm{P}}\leq-\overline{g}_{\mathrm{P}}(\pi,e),\;e\in{\rm E}\right\}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\left\{\left(g(x,i),\;z_{\mathrm{P}}\right):\;\;z_{\mathrm{P}}\leq-g_{\mathrm{P}}(x,i),\;i\in{\rm I}\right\}

are closed convex sets.

Remark 2.12
.

These assumptions will be used in the same spirit as in
Filippov 1962
[
26
]
and
Roxin 1962
[
48
]
. A simple situation where
2.11
is verified is when: the maps
a\mapsto b(t,x,\pi,a)
and
m\mapsto\left(K_{b,\pi,m},\;\left(\overline{b},\overline{L}\right)\left(t,[\pi(t),m],e\right)\right)
are affine and,
(a,m,e,i,u)\mapsto\left(L(t,x,\pi,a),\;-\overline{L}_{\mathrm{P}}\left(t,[\pi(t),m],e\right),\;-\overline{g}_{\mathrm{P}}(\pi,e),-g_{\mathrm{P}}(x,i)\right)
is concave
(
we considered the product order to check the concavity inequality
)
.

Theorem 2.13
.

Under
2.1
and
2.11
, for any contract
\mathfrak{C}=(\phi,\xi,\aleph)
, the set
{\rm MFG}[\mathfrak{C}]
is non–empty.

Remark 2.14
.

Theorem

2.13
is in fact an extension of
Djete 2020
[
13
, Theorem 7.2.4.]
, however the proof given here is simpler and does not use any discretization arguments as is usually the case with weak solutions of
MFG
with common noise
(
see
[
8
]
)
. Putting aside the dependence on the contract, the assumptions considered in
Theorem

2.13
seems general in comparison to the literature regarding the wellposedness of
MFG
solutions
(
MFG
of controls solutions to be more precise, see for instance
Carmona and Zeng 2022
[
7
]
,
Jaber et al. 2023
[
33
]
)
.

Theorem 2.15
.

Let
2.1
and
2.11
hold true. If
R_{0}
is s.t.
\Xi
is non–empty then there exist progressively Borel measurable map
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{\cal C}_{{\cal W}}\times{\cal P}_{A}\ni(t,x,\pi,m)\mapsto\widehat{\alpha}(t,x,\pi,m)\in{\rm E}\times A
, a contract
\mathfrak{C}^{\star}=(\phi^{\star},\xi^{\star},\aleph^{\star})\in\Xi
and
\left(\alpha^{\star},\overline{\mu}^{\star}\right)\in\underline{\rm MFG}[\mathfrak{C}^{\star}]
verifying:

\displaystyle\alpha^{\star}(t,X^{\alpha^{\star}}_{t},\overline{\mu}^{\star},\aleph^{\star})=\widehat{\alpha}(t,X^{\alpha^{\star}}_{t},\mu^{\star},\overline{\mu}^{\star}_{t})\;\;\;\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\mbox{--a.e.},~~~~\mbox{\rm and}~~~~V_{\mathrm{P}}=J_{\mathrm{P}}^{{\alpha^{\star}},\overline{\mu}^{\star}}(\mathfrak{C}^{\star}).

We will say that
\mathfrak{C}^{\star}=(\phi^{\star},\xi^{\star},\aleph^{\star})
solves the problem
V_{\mathrm{P}}
.

Remark 2.16
.

(i)
Compared to the results known in the literature of Principal–Agent,
Theorem

2.15
provides existence results under general assumptions
(
see
[
23
,
2
]

)
. The condition
``
R_{0}
is s.t.
\Xi
is non–empty
"
may seem restrictive but it is actually a reasonable condition. Indeed, if the reservation utility is unreasonably high, for instance
\overline{L},L,g
and
\overline{g}
are all negative and
R_{0}>0
, the problem has no interest since there will not be a solution. Therefore, this condition is there to avoid these kinds of situations and to make sure that the problem is worth solving. Given the maps
\overline{L},L,g
and
\overline{g}
, notice that sometimes it is not difficult to get
R_{0}
s.t.
\Xi
is non–empty. For example, if
(\overline{L},L,g,\overline{g})
are lower bounded, it is straightforward to check that for any
R_{0}\in\left(\inf(\overline{L}+L+g+\overline{g}),-\infty\right)
,
\Xi
is non–empty.

(ii)
When we see the statement of
Theorem

2.15
, it might lead to think that the optimal control
\alpha^{\star}
does not need to use the information of the contract
\aleph^{\star}
. This is not the case. Since the process
\overline{\mu}^{\star}
achieves an optimum when
\aleph^{\star}
is fixed, it contains part of the information of the contract
\aleph^{\star}
. The shape of the optimal contract given in
Theorem

2.15
provides some insight into the type of information needed to reach the optimum. It should be mentioned that the form of the coefficients plays an important role, in particular the separability of the variables
(m,e)
on the one hand and
(x,\pi,a)
on the other hand.

We now give some convergence results. In the next Theorem, we first provide the characterization of the limits of sequence of contracts and (approximate) MFG solutions. Second, we state the approximation of any MFG solution associated to a contract by a sequence of contracts and of (approximate) MFG solutions.

Theorem 2.17
.

Let
2.1
hold true.

•

We consider a sequence of non–negative number
(\varepsilon_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
s.t.
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\varepsilon_{\ell}=0.
Let
(\alpha^{\ell},\overline{\mu}^{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
and
(\mathfrak{C}^{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}=(\phi^{\ell},\xi^{\ell},\aleph^{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
be sequences such that
(\alpha^{\ell},\overline{\mu}^{\ell})\in\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C}^{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell}]
for each
\ell\geq 1
then
\left(J^{\alpha^{\ell},\overline{\mu}^{\ell}}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathfrak{C}^{\ell}),J^{\mathfrak{C}^{\ell}}_{A,\overline{\mu}^{\ell}}(\alpha^{\ell})\right)_{\ell\geq 1}
is relatively compact and for any convergent sequence
(\ell_{j})_{j\geq 1}
, under
2.11
, there exist
\mathfrak{C}
and
(\alpha,\overline{\mu})
s.t.
(\alpha,\overline{\mu})\in\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C}]
,

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}J^{\mathfrak{C}^{\ell_{j}}}_{A,\overline{\mu}^{\ell_{j}}}({\alpha^{\ell_{j}}})\leq J^{\mathfrak{C}}_{A,\overline{\mu}}({\alpha})~~~~~\mbox{\rm and}~~~~\lim_{j\to\infty}J^{\alpha^{\ell_{j}},\overline{\mu}^{\ell_{j}}}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathfrak{C}^{\ell_{j}})\leq J^{\alpha,\overline{\mu}}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathfrak{C}).

•

Let
\mathfrak{C}=(\phi,\xi,\aleph)
be a contract and
(\alpha,\overline{\mu})\in\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C}]
. There exist a sequence of non–negative numbers
(\varepsilon_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
s.t.
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\varepsilon_{\ell}=0,
and a sequence
(\alpha^{\ell},\overline{\mu}^{\ell},\phi^{\ell},\xi^{\ell},\aleph^{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
s.t. for each
\ell\geq 1
,
\mathfrak{C}^{\ell}=(\phi^{\ell},\xi^{\ell},\aleph^{\ell})
is a contract,
(\alpha^{\ell},\overline{\mu}^{\ell})\in\mbox{{\rm MFG}}_{S}[\mathfrak{C}^{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell}]
and

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}J^{\mathfrak{C}^{\ell}}_{A,\overline{\mu}^{\ell}}(\alpha^{\ell})=J^{\mathfrak{C}}_{A,\overline{\mu}}({\alpha})~~~~~\mbox{\rm and}~~~~\lim_{\ell\to\infty}J^{\alpha^{\ell},\overline{\mu}^{\ell}}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathfrak{C}^{\ell})=J^{\alpha,\overline{\mu}}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathfrak{C}).

Remark 2.18
.

To the best of our knowledge,
Theorem

2.17
is the first of this type in the literature of contract theory. From the point of view of
MFG
theory, this result can be seen as the characterization of
MFG
solutions associated with varying parameters. This appears to be new. In the proofs of this result, we provide more general convergence results than those mentioned in the Theorem by using the notion of
``
relaxed
"
controls. We have chosen this presentation for its ease of reading and because it gives the main information that we wish to share.

By using
Theorem

2.17
, we are able to give a convergence result on the problem of the Principal. In simple words, the problem in which the Principal considers contracts leading to approximate strong MFG solutions for the representative Agent is close to the problem in which the Principal offers contracts leading to MFG solutions for the representative Agent.

Corollary 2.19
.

Let
2.1
be true and
R_{0}
s.t.
\Xi
is non–empty. Then, for each
(\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0})\in(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty),
the set
\Xi_{S}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
is non–empty and we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0,\varepsilon>0}\lim_{\varepsilon_{0}\to 0,\varepsilon_{0}>0}V_{\mathrm{P}}^{S}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]=V_{\mathrm{P}}.

Besides, for each
\delta>0
, there exist
\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
(\alpha,\overline{\mu})
and
\mathfrak{C}
s.t.
\mathfrak{C}\in\Xi_{S}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
,
(\alpha,\overline{\mu})\in\underline{\rm MFG}_{S}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
and
V_{\mathrm{P}}\leq J^{\alpha,\overline{\mu}}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathfrak{C})+\delta
.

The following Theorem starts by giving an understanding of the limits of sequence of contracts and approximate Nash equilibria. Next, it provides an approximation of any MFG solution associated to a contract by a sequence of approximate Nash equilibria associated to a sequence of contracts for the problem of Principal and
n
Agents.

Theorem 2.20
.

Let
2.1
hold true.

•

Let
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of non–negative numbers s.t.
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\varepsilon_{n}=0
. We consider a sequence
(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n},\phi^{n},\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})
s.t.
\mathfrak{C}^{n}=(\phi^{n},\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})
is a contract and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}\in\mbox{\rm NE}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon_{n}]
for each
n\geq 1
. The sequence

\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}J^{\mathfrak{C}^{n}}_{n,i}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}),~~J_{n,\mathrm{P}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}}}(\mathfrak{C}^{n})\right)_{n\geq 1}

is relatively compact and for any convergent sub–sequence
(n_{j})_{j\geq 1}
, under
2.11
, there exist
\mathfrak{C}=(\phi,\xi,\aleph)
and
(\alpha,\overline{\mu})\in\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C}]
verifying

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}}J^{\mathfrak{C}^{n_{j}}}_{n_{j},i}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n_{j}})\leq J^{\mathfrak{C}}_{A,\overline{\mu}}({\alpha})~~~~~\mbox{\rm and}~~~~\lim_{j\to\infty}J_{n_{j},\mathrm{P}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n_{j}}}}(\mathfrak{C}^{n_{j}})\leq J_{\mathrm{P}}^{\alpha,\overline{\mu}}(\mathfrak{C}).

•

Let
\mathfrak{C}=(\phi,\xi,\aleph)
be a contract and
(\alpha,\overline{\mu})\in\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C}]
. There exist a sequence of non–negative numbers
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 1}
s.t.
\lim_{n\to\infty}\varepsilon_{n}=0,
and a sequence
(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n},\phi^{n},\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})_{\ell\geq 1}
s.t. for each
n\geq 1
,
\mathfrak{C}^{n}=(\phi^{n},\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})
is a distributed contract,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}\in{\rm NE}^{\rm dist}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon_{n}]

and we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}J^{\mathfrak{C}^{n}}_{n,i}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n})=J^{\mathfrak{C}}_{A,\overline{\mu}}({\alpha})~~~~~\mbox{\rm and}~~~~\lim_{n\to\infty}J_{n,\mathrm{P}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}}}(\mathfrak{C}^{n})=J_{\mathrm{P}}^{\alpha,\overline{\mu}}(\mathfrak{C}).

Remark 2.21
.

Similarly to
Theorem

2.17
,
Theorem

2.20
seems to be the first of this kind in the literature of contract theory. From a
MFG
theory perspective, it is worth mentioning that, since the map
(\xi^{n},\aleph^{n})
depends on
(X^{1},\dots,X^{n})
in a general way, This Theorem goes beyond the classical
MFG
framework. See
Lacker et al. 2022
[
40
]
and
Jackson and Lacker 2023
[
34
]
for related analysis beyond the classical mean–field dependence framework. Some classical results of
MFG
literature are also contained in this Theorem. Also as in
Theorem

2.17
, the results presented could be more general using
``
relaxed
"
controls, this presentation was chosen for ease of reading.

In the same spirit as in
Corollary

2.19
, by the use of
Theorem

2.20
, the following result simply shows that the problem of the Principal with
n
agents converges when
n
goes to infinity to the problem of the Principal with the representative agent.

Corollary 2.22
.

Let
R_{0}
be s.t.
\Xi
is non–empty and
2.1
hold true. For each
(\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0})\in(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
, there exists
\overline{n}\geq 1
s.t. for each
n\geq\overline{n}
,
\Xi^{\rm dist}_{n}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]
is non–empty, and we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lim_{\varepsilon_{0}\to 0}\lim_{n\to\infty}V_{n,\mathrm{P}}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lim_{\varepsilon_{0}\to 0}\lim_{n\to\infty}V^{\rm dist}_{n,\mathrm{P}}[\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}]=V_{\mathrm{P}}.

Also, for each
\delta>0
, there exist
n\geq 1
,
(\varepsilon_{n},\varepsilon_{0,n})\in(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}
and
\mathfrak{C}^{n}
s.t.
\mathfrak{C}^{n}
is a distributed contract,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}\in\underline{\rm NE}^{\rm dist}[\mathfrak{C}^{n},\varepsilon_{n},\varepsilon_{0,n}]
and
V_{n,\mathrm{P}}[\varepsilon_{n},\varepsilon_{0,n}]\leq J_{n,\mathrm{P}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{n}}}(\mathfrak{C}^{n})+\delta
.

Remark 2.23
.

It is worth emphasizing that the previous result point out the fact that when the number of Agents
n
is large enough, considering distributed contracts is equivalent to considering contracts of a general type for the Principal when he faces
n
Agents in competition.

3
Proof of the main results

This whole section is dedicated to the proofs of our main results. We start in
Section

3.1
by reformulating our problem in an adequate setup for the proofs. Then, in
Section

3.2
, we give the proofs of the characterization of the limits of sequence of approximate Nash equilibria and approximate MFG solutions. In
Section

3.3
, we show how to construct a sequence of approximate Nash equilibria from a MFG solution. Next, we show why the set of MFG solutions considered in this paper is non–empty in
Section

3.4
. Finally,
Section

3.5
presents the actual proofs of the main Theorem/Corollary stated in the paper.

3.1
Reformulation of the MFG

Denote by
\mathbb{M}:=\mathbb{M}\left({\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}\right)
the collection of all finite (Borel) measures
q(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)
on
{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}\times[0,T]
,
whose marginal distribution on
[0,T]
is the Lebesgue measure
\mathrm{d}s

i.e.,
q(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e,\mathrm{d}s)=q(s,\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}s
for a measurable family
(q(s,\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e))_{s\in[0,T]}
of Borel probability measures on
{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}.

For
\Lambda
the canonical element on
\mathbb{M}
and the canonical filtration
\mathbb{F}^{\Lambda}=({\cal F}^{\Lambda}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
on
\mathbb{M}
defined by

{\cal F}^{\Lambda}_{t}:=\sigma\big\{\Lambda(C\times[0,s])~:\forall s\leq t,\;C\in{\cal B}({\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E})\big\},

we have the disintegration property:
q(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e,\mathrm{d}t)=q(t,\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}t
, with a version of the disintegration
such that
(t,q)\mapsto q(t,\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)
is
\mathbb{F}^{\Lambda}
–predictable.

We introduce the canonical space
\overline{\Omega}:={\cal C}\times{\cal C}\times{\cal C}\times\mathbb{M}\times{\cal C}_{{\cal W}}
and its associated canonical variables
(X^{\prime},W,B,R,\mu).
Then, the canonical filtration
\overline{\mathbb{F}}=(\overline{{\cal F}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is defined by: for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\overline{{\cal F}}_{t}:=\sigma\big\{X^{\prime}_{t\wedge\cdot},W_{t\wedge\cdot},B_{t\wedge\cdot},R_{t\wedge\cdot},\mu_{t\wedge\cdot}\big\}

with
R_{t\wedge\cdot}
denotes the restriction of
R
on
{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}\times[0,t]
(see definition
1.1
). Notice that we can choose a version of the disintegration
R(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e,\mathrm{d}t)=R_{t}(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}t
with
(R_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is a
{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}
–valued
\overline{\mathbb{F}}
–predictable process.
Let us also introduce the
common noise
filtration
{\overline{\mathbb{G}}}:=(\overline{{\cal G}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
by: for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\overline{{\cal G}}_{t}:=\sigma\big\{B_{t\wedge\cdot},R_{t\wedge\cdot},\mu_{t\wedge\cdot}\big\}.

Definition 3.1

(control rule)
.

We say that
\mathrm{P}\in{\cal P}(\overline{\Omega})
belongs to
\overline{{\cal P}}
i.e. a control rule if:

(i)

(W,B)
is an
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
–valued
\mathrm{P}
–Brownian motion and the distribution of
X^{\prime}_{0}
under
\mathrm{P}
is
\nu

(ii)

There exists a progressively Borel measurable map
\Lambda^{\prime}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}\to{\cal P}(A)
such that the tuple
(X^{\prime},W,B,R,\mu)
satisfies:
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\otimes\mathrm{d}t
–a.e.
(\omega,t)
,
R_{t}\left(\left\{(m,e):\;\mu_{t}=m(\mathrm{d}x,A)\right\}\right)=1
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X^{\prime}_{t}=\int_{{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}}\overline{b}\left(t,m,e\right)R_{t}(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)+\int_{A}b\left(t,X_{t}^{\prime},\mu,a\right)\Lambda^{\prime}\left(t,X^{\prime}_{t},R\right)(\mathrm{d}a)\;\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,X_{t}^{\prime})\;\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\sigma_{0}\;\mathrm{d}B_{t}\;\;\mathrm{P}\mbox{--a.e.}

(3.1)

(iii)

In addition,
the variables
(B,R,\mu)
, W and
X^{\prime}_{0}
are
\mathrm{P}
–independent.

For Polish spaces
\Theta
and
\Theta^{\prime}
, we will say that
{\rm K}
belongs to
{\cal K}(\Theta,\Theta^{\prime})
if
{\rm K}
is a Borel map from
\Theta
to
{\cal P}(\Theta^{\prime})
. If for
{\rm K}\in{\cal K}(\Theta,\Theta^{\prime})
, we have
{\rm K}(\theta)=\delta_{\kappa(\theta)}(\mathrm{d}i)
for some Borel map
\kappa:\Theta\to\Theta^{\prime}
, we will say
{\rm K}\in{\cal K}_{\rm 0}(\Theta,\Theta^{\prime})
. For any
(\gamma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},{\rm r},\pi,\Phi,\mathfrak{Z})\in\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A)\times{\cal C}_{{\cal W}}\times\mathbb{M}\times{\cal C}_{{\cal W}}\times{\cal K}(\mathbb{R},{\rm I})\times{\cal K}(\mathbb{M},{\rm E})
, one defines

\displaystyle{\rm J}^{\Phi,\mathfrak{Z}}_{A}(\gamma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},{\rm r},\pi)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{T}\langle\overline{L}\left(t,\cdot,\cdot\right),{\rm r}(t)\rangle+\langle L\left(t,\cdot,\pi,\cdot\right),\gamma^{\prime}(t)\rangle\;\;\mathrm{d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}\times{\rm I}}\overline{g}(x,i)\Phi(x)(\mathrm{d}i)\pi^{\prime}(T)(\mathrm{d}x)+\int_{{\rm E}}g(\pi,e)\mathfrak{Z}({\rm r})(\mathrm{d}e).

For any
\mathrm{P}\in{\cal P}(\overline{\Omega})
, we set,
\mathrm{P}
–a.e.,
\mu^{\prime}_{t}:={\cal L}(X_{t}^{\prime}|\overline{{\cal G}}_{t})\;\mbox{for all}\;t\in[0,T]
and when
\mathrm{P}\in\overline{{\cal P}}
,

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime}:=\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{P}}\left[\delta_{X_{t}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}x)\Lambda^{\prime}\left(t,X^{\prime}_{t},R\right)(\mathrm{d}a)|\overline{{\cal G}}_{t}\right]\mathrm{d}t.

Remark 3.2
.

(i)
To make an analogy with the definition given in
Section

2.2
, the canonical variables
(X^{\prime},W,B,R,\mu)
will play the
``
role
"
of the variables
(X^{\beta},W,B,R,\mu)
on
(\Omega,\mathbb{H},\mathbb{P})
for some
\beta\in{\cal A}
.

(ii)
Notice that, for any
\mathrm{P}\in\overline{{\cal P}}
, since
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\otimes\mathrm{d}t
–a.e.
(\omega,t)
,
R_{t}\left(\left\{(m,e):\;\mu_{t}=m(\mathrm{d}x,A)\right\}\right)=1
, we can see that
\mu_{t}
is a Borel map of
R_{t}
.

(iii)
For
\mathrm{P}\in{\cal P}(\overline{\Omega})
, we will
abuse the terminology
and say that
\mathrm{P}\in\overline{{\cal P}}
if we can find
\mathrm{P}^{\prime}\in\overline{{\cal P}}
s.t.
\mathrm{P}\circ\left(\mu^{\prime},X_{0}^{\prime},W,B,R,\mu\right)^{-1}=\mathrm{P}^{\prime}\circ\left(\mu^{\prime},X_{0}^{\prime},W,B,R,\mu\right)^{-1}
. In other words, our variables of interest are
(\mu^{\prime},X_{0}^{\prime},W,B,R,\mu)
. The process
X^{\prime}
is here for simplifying the presentation.

For any
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
(t,x,\pi,a)
, in order to accommodate the notation, we write
(\varphi b)(t,x,\pi,a)
instead of
\varphi(x)b(t,x,\pi,a)
.

Definition 3.3
.

(MFG solution)

For each
\varepsilon\geq 0
, we say that
\mathrm{P}^{\star}\in\overline{{\cal P}}
is an
\varepsilon
–
MFG
solution if:
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}^{\star}\otimes\mathrm{d}t
–a.e.,
\mu_{t}=\mu_{t}^{\prime}={\cal L}^{\mathrm{P}^{\star}}(X^{\prime}_{t}|\overline{{\cal G}}_{T})
, for any
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{P}^{\star}}\left[\int_{A}\left(\varphi b,\;\;L\right)\left(t,X^{\prime}_{t},\mu,a\right)\Lambda^{\prime}(t,X^{\prime}_{t},R)(\mathrm{d}a)|\overline{{\cal G}}_{T}\right]=\int_{{\cal P}_{A}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A}\left(\varphi b,\;\;L\right)\left(t,x,\mu,a\right)m(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}a)R_{t}(\mathrm{d}m,{\rm E}),

(3.2)

and there exists
(\Phi,\mathfrak{Z})\in{\cal K}(\mathbb{R},{\rm I})\times{\cal K}(\mathbb{M},{\rm E})
s.t. for every
\mathrm{P}\in\overline{{\cal P}}
verifying
{\cal L}^{\mathrm{P}^{\star}}\big(B,R\big)={\cal L}^{\mathrm{P}}\big(B,R\big)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{P}^{\star}}\big[{\rm J}^{\Phi,\mathfrak{Z}}_{A}\left(\Gamma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},R,\mu\right)\big]\geq\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{P}}\big[{\rm J}^{\Phi,\mathfrak{Z}}_{A}\left(\Gamma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},R,\mu\right)\big]-\varepsilon.

(3.3)

We will say
\mathrm{P}^{\star}
is an
\varepsilon
–MFG solution associated to
(\Phi,\mathfrak{Z})
.
We denote by
\overline{{\cal P}}_{\rm\varepsilon\mbox{-}mfg}
the set of all MFG solutions. When
\varepsilon=0
, we will simply write
\overline{{\cal P}}_{\rm mfg}
.

We now introduce
\overline{{\cal P}}_{0,\;\rm\varepsilon\mbox{-}mfg}
by

\displaystyle\overline{{\cal P}}_{0,\;\rm\varepsilon\mbox{-}mfg}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{\mathrm{P}\in\overline{{\cal P}}_{\rm\varepsilon\mbox{-}mfg},\;\exists\;(\overline{m}_{t},\overline{e}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\;\mbox{and}\;\alpha\;\mbox{s.t.}\;\mathbb{P}\mbox{--a.e.}\;R=\delta_{(\overline{m}_{t},\overline{e}_{t})}(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\;\Lambda^{\prime}(t,X_{t}^{\prime},R)(\mathrm{d}a)\mathrm{d}t=\delta_{\alpha(t,X_{t}^{\prime},\mu,\overline{m}_{t})}(\mathrm{d}a)\mathrm{d}t\;\mbox{and}\;(\Phi,\mathfrak{Z})\in{\cal K}_{\rm 0}(\mathbb{R},{\rm I})\times{\cal K}_{\rm 0}(\mathbb{M},{\rm E})\Big\}.

Remark 3.4
.

Let us observe that: by taking the conditional expectation w.r.t.
\overline{{\cal G}}_{T}
in (
3.1
), for
\mathrm{P}\in\overline{{\cal P}}_{\rm\varepsilon\mbox{-}mfg}
,
\mathrm{P}
–a.e., for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{0}B_{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}x\mu_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}x\nu(\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{0}^{t}\;\int_{{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}}\left[\overline{b}\left(r,m,e\right)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A}b\left(r,x,\mu,a\right)m(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}a)\right]\;R_{r}(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}r.

Since
\sigma_{0}
is invertible, similarly to
[
15
, Lemma 3.4.]
, there exists a progressively Borel measurable map
[0,T]\times\mathbb{M}\times C([0,T];{\cal P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\ni(t,{\rm r},\pi)\mapsto\varphi(t,{\rm r},\pi)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
continuous in all variables s.t. for any
\mathrm{P}\in\overline{{\cal P}}_{\rm\varepsilon\mbox{-}mfg}
, one has, for all
t\in[0,T]
,
B_{t}=\varphi\left(t,R,\mu\right)
,
\mathrm{P}
–a.e. Furthermore, since
\mu_{t}
is a Borel map of
R_{t}
, the Brownian motion
B
can be seen as a progressively Borel map of
R
.

For each
({\rm r},\pi)
, we introduce

\displaystyle{\rm J}_{\mathrm{P}}^{{\rm r},\pi}\left(\Phi,\mathfrak{Z}\right):=\int_{{\rm E}}U\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Upsilon(x)\;\pi(T)(\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{{\rm I}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}g_{\mathrm{P}}\left(x,i\right)\Phi(x)(\mathrm{d}i)\pi(T)(\mathrm{d}x)-\overline{g}_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\pi,e\right)-\int_{0}^{T}\langle\overline{L}_{\mathrm{P}}(t,\cdot,\cdot),{\rm r}(t)\rangle\;\mathrm{d}t\right)\mathfrak{Z}({\rm r})(\mathrm{d}e).

Simply put, the next Proposition shows that, when
2.11
is satisfied, we can use the set
\overline{{\cal P}}_{0,\;\rm\varepsilon\mbox{-}mfg}
instead of
\overline{{\cal P}}_{\rm\varepsilon\mbox{-}mfg}
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Under
2.11
, for any
\mathrm{P}^{\star}\in\overline{{\cal P}}_{\rm\varepsilon\mbox{-}mfg}
associated to
(\Phi,\mathfrak{Z})
, there exists
\widetilde{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}\in\overline{{\cal P}}_{0,\;\rm\varepsilon\mbox{-}mfg}
associated to
(\widetilde{\Phi},\widetilde{\mathfrak{Z}})
s.t.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}}\big[{\rm J}^{\Phi,\mathfrak{Z}}_{A}(\Gamma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},R,\mu)\big]\leq\mathbb{E}^{\widetilde{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}}\big[{\rm J}^{\tilde{\Phi},\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}}_{A}(\Gamma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},R,\mu)\big]\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\mathbb{E}^{{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}}\big[{\rm J}^{R,\mu}_{\mathrm{P}}(\Phi,\mathfrak{Z})\big]\leq\mathbb{E}^{\widetilde{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}}\big[{\rm J}^{R,\mu}_{\mathrm{P}}(\widetilde{\Phi},\widetilde{\mathfrak{Z}})\big].

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{P}^{\star}\in\overline{{\cal P}}_{\rm\varepsilon\mbox{-}mfg}
. We use the same ideas of
Filippov 1962
[
26
]
and
Roxin 1962
[
48
]
. Under
2.11
, by applying
Proposition

A.3
and using the fact that
\mu_{t}
is a Borel map of
R_{t}
, first, we can find progressively Borel measurable maps
\overline{m}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{M}\to{\cal P}_{A}
and
\overline{e}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{M}\to{\rm E}
s.t.
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}^{\star}\otimes\mathrm{d}t
–a.e.
\overline{m}(t,R)(\mathrm{d}x,A)=\mu_{t}
,

\displaystyle\int_{{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}}\overline{L}(t,m,e)R_{t}(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)=\overline{L}\left(t,\overline{m}(t,R),\overline{e}\left(t,R\right)\right),\;\int_{{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}}\langle L\;(t,\cdot,\mu,\cdot),m\rangle R_{t}(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)\leq\langle L\;(t,\cdot,\mu,\cdot),\overline{m}(t,R)\rangle,

\displaystyle-\int_{{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}}\overline{L}_{\mathrm{P}}(t,m,e)R_{t}(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)\leq-\overline{L}_{\mathrm{P}}\left(t,\overline{m}(t,R),\overline{e}\left(t,R\right)\right)

and for
\varphi\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\int_{{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}}\left(\overline{b}(t,m,e),\;\langle\varphi b\;(t,\cdot,\mu,\cdot),m\rangle\right)R_{t}(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)=\left(\overline{b}\left(t,\overline{m}(t,R),\overline{e}\left(t,R\right)\right),\;\langle\varphi b\;(t,\cdot,\mu,\cdot),\overline{m}(t,R)\rangle\right).

(3.4)

Second, let
\overline{R}:=\delta_{\left(\overline{m}(t,R),\overline{e}(t,R)\right)}(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}t
, we get Borel maps
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{M}\ni(x,{\rm r})\mapsto(\phi(x),z({\rm r}))\in{\rm I}\times{\rm E}
s.t.

\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{P}}\left[\int_{{\rm E}}\overline{g}(\mu,e))\mathfrak{Z}(R)(\mathrm{d}e)|\overline{R}\right]=\overline{g}(\mu,z\left(\overline{R}\right))\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\int_{{\rm I}}g(x,i))\Phi(x)(\mathrm{d}i)=g(x,\phi\left(x\right)),

\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{P}}\left[-\int_{{\rm E}}\overline{g}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mu,e))\mathfrak{Z}(R)(\mathrm{d}e)|\overline{R}\right]\leq-\overline{g}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mu,z\left(\overline{R}\right))\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;-\int_{{\rm I}}g_{\mathrm{P}}(x,i))\Phi(x)(\mathrm{d}i)\leq-g_{\mathrm{P}}(x,\phi\left(x\right)).

Notice that, since the utility map
U:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is non–decreasing and concave, we have,
\mathrm{P}
–a.e.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{P}}\left[{\rm J}_{\mathrm{P}}^{R,\mu}\left(\Phi,\mathfrak{Z}\right)|\overline{R}\right]\leq U\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Upsilon(x)\;\mu_{T}(\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g_{\mathrm{P}}\left(x,\phi(x)\right)\mu_{T}(\mathrm{d}x)-\overline{g}_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\pi,z(\overline{R})\right)-\int_{0}^{T}\overline{L}_{\mathrm{P}}\left(t,\overline{m}(t,R),\overline{e}(t,R)\right)\;\mathrm{d}t\right).

Lastly, by the same
Proposition

A.3
, there exists a Borel progressively measurable
\alpha:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{\cal C}_{{\cal W}}\times{\cal P}_{A}\to A
s.t.
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}^{\star}\otimes\mathrm{d}t
–a.e. for
\varphi\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{P}^{\star}}\left[\int_{A}\varphi(X_{t}^{\prime})b\left(t,X^{\prime}_{t},\mu,a\right)\Lambda^{\prime}\left(t,X_{t}^{\prime},R\right)(\mathrm{d}a)|\overline{{\cal G}}_{t}\right]=\int_{{\cal P}_{A}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A}\varphi(x)b(t,x,\mu,a)m(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}a)R_{t}(\mathrm{d}m,{\rm E})

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi b\;(t,\cdot,\mu,\cdot),\overline{m}(t,R)\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)b\left(t,x,\mu,\alpha\left(t,x,\mu,\overline{m}(t,R)\right)\right)\mu_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)

(3.5)

and

\displaystyle\left\langle L\left(t,X^{\prime}_{t},\mu,\cdot\right),\;\Lambda^{\prime}\left(t,X_{t}^{\prime},R\right)\right\rangle\leq L\left(t,X^{\prime}_{t},\mu,\alpha\left(t,X^{\prime}_{t},\mu,\overline{m}(t,R)\right)\right).

We define
X^{\alpha}
the weak solution of:
\mathrm{P}^{\star}
–a.e.,
X^{\alpha}_{0}=X^{\prime}_{0}
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X^{\alpha}_{t}=\overline{b}\left(t,\overline{m}(t,R),\overline{e}\left(t,R\right)\right)\mathrm{d}t+b\left(t,X^{\alpha}_{t},\mu,\alpha(t,X^{\alpha}_{t},\mu,\overline{m}(t,R))\right)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,X^{\alpha}_{t})\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\sigma_{0}\mathrm{d}B_{t}.

We set
\widetilde{\mu}_{t}:={\cal L}^{\mathrm{P}^{\star}}(X^{\alpha}_{t}|R,B)
,
\overline{\mu}_{t}:=\overline{m}(t,R)
,
\mathrm{P}^{\star}
–a.e. for all
t\in[0,T]
,
R^{\alpha}:=\delta_{\left(\overline{\mu}_{t},\;\overline{e}_{t}\right)}(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}t
and
\overline{e}_{t}:=\overline{e}(t,R)
. Thanks to the equalities in (
3.4
) and (
3.1
), we can check that
\widetilde{\mu}=\mu

\mathrm{P}^{\star}
–a.e. Let us set
\widetilde{\Phi}(x)(\mathrm{d}i):=\delta_{\phi(x)}(\mathrm{d}i)
,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Z}}({\rm r})(\mathrm{d}e):=\delta_{z({\rm r})}(\mathrm{d}e)
, and define the probability
\widetilde{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}
defined by

\widetilde{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}:=\mathrm{P}^{\star}\circ\left(X^{\alpha},W,B,R^{\alpha},\widetilde{\mu}\right)^{-1}.

By combining the previous results, it is straightforward that
\mathbb{E}^{{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}}\big[{\rm J}^{\Phi,\mathfrak{Z}}_{A}(\Gamma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},R,\mu)\big]\leq\mathbb{E}^{\widetilde{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}}\big[{\rm J}^{\tilde{\Phi},\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}}_{A}(\Gamma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},R,\mu)\big]
and
\mathbb{E}^{{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}}\big[{\rm J}^{R,\mu}_{\mathrm{P}}(\Phi,\mathfrak{Z})\big]\leq\mathbb{E}^{\widetilde{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}}\big[{\rm J}^{R,\mu}_{\mathrm{P}}(\widetilde{\Phi},\widetilde{\mathfrak{Z}})\big]
. Also, for
\widetilde{\mathrm{P}}\in\overline{{\cal P}}
s.t.
{\cal L}^{\widetilde{\mathrm{P}}}(B,R)={\cal L}^{\widetilde{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}}(B,R)
, we can find
\mathrm{P}\in\overline{{\cal P}}
verifying
{\cal L}^{{\mathrm{P}}}(B,R)={\cal L}^{{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}}(B,R)
s.t.

\mathbb{E}^{\widetilde{\mathrm{P}}}\big[{\rm J}^{\tilde{\Phi},\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}}_{A}(\Gamma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},R,\mu)\big]=\mathbb{E}^{{\mathrm{P}}}\big[{\rm J}^{\Phi,\mathfrak{Z}}_{A}(\Gamma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},R,\mu)\big]\leq\mathbb{E}^{{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}}\big[{\rm J}^{\Phi,\mathfrak{Z}}_{A}(\Gamma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},R,\mu)\big]\leq\mathbb{E}^{\widetilde{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}}\big[{\rm J}^{\tilde{\Phi},\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}}_{A}(\Gamma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},R,\mu)\big].

Therefore, we obtain that
\widetilde{\mathrm{P}}^{\star}\in\overline{{\cal P}}_{0,\;\rm\varepsilon\mbox{-}mfg}
.

∎

We recall that the progressively Borel map
\Lambda^{\prime}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}\to{\cal P}(A)
given in
Definition

3.1
has to be seen as a control. Under
2.1
, the next result shows that, by an approximation result, we can replace
\Lambda^{\prime}(t,X^{\prime}_{t},R)(\mathrm{d}a)
by
\delta_{\lambda^{\prime}(t,X^{\prime}_{t},R)}(\mathrm{d}a)
for some progressively Borel map
\lambda^{\prime}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}\to A
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
2.1
hold true. Let
\mathrm{P}\in\overline{{\cal P}}
s.t.
\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}
–a.e.
B_{t}=\varphi(t,R)
for some progressively Borel map
\varphi
. Then, there exist a sequence of Borel maps
(\lambda^{\prime\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
, a sequence of probabilities
(\mathrm{P}^{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}\subset\overline{{\cal P}}
s.t. for each
\ell\geq 1
,
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}\ni(t,x,{\rm r})\mapsto\lambda^{\prime\ell}(t,x,{\rm r})\in A
is Lipschitz in
(x,{\rm r})
uniformly in
t
,

\mathrm{P}^{\ell}\left(\Lambda^{\prime}(t,X^{\prime}_{t},R)(\mathrm{d}a)\mathrm{d}t=\delta_{\lambda^{\prime\ell}(t,X_{t}^{\prime},R)}(\mathrm{d}a)\mathrm{d}t\right)=1

and

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mathrm{P}^{\ell}\left(\mu^{\prime},{\rm K}(y)(\mathrm{d}i)\mu_{T}^{\prime}(\mathrm{d}y),\mu,R,B\right)^{-1}=\mathrm{P}\left(\mu^{\prime},{\rm K}(y)(\mathrm{d}i)\mu_{T}^{\prime}(\mathrm{d}y),\mu,R,B\right)^{-1}\;\;\mbox{in }{\cal W}_{p}\;\;\mbox{for any }{\rm K}\in{\cal K}(\mathbb{R}^{d},{\rm I}).

Proof.

For
\mathrm{P}\in\overline{{\cal P}}
. Recall that,
\mathrm{P}
–a.e.

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X^{\prime}_{t}=\int_{{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}}\overline{b}(t,m,e)R_{t}(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)+\int_{A}b\left(t,X_{t}^{\prime},\mu,a\right)\Lambda^{\prime}\left(t,X_{t}^{\prime},R\right)(\mathrm{d}a)\;\;\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,X_{t}^{\prime})\;\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\sigma_{0}\;\mathrm{d}B_{t}.

By easy extension of
[
15
, Proposition A.5.]
(see also
[
15
, Lemma 3.5.]
), there exists a sequence
(\lambda^{\prime\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}\subset{\cal A}
s.t. for each
\ell\geq 1
,
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}\ni(t,x,{\rm r})\mapsto\lambda^{\prime\ell}(t,x,{\rm r})\in A
is Lipschitz in
(x,{\rm r})
uniformly in
t
and if
X^{\prime\ell}
is the solution of

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X^{\prime\ell}_{t}=\int_{{\cal P}_{A}\times{\rm E}}\overline{b}(t,m,e)R_{t}(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)+b\left(t,X^{\prime\ell}_{t},\mu,\lambda^{\prime\ell}\left(t,X^{\prime\ell}_{t},R\right)\right)\;\;\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,X^{\prime\ell}_{t})\;\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\sigma_{0}\;\mathrm{d}B_{t}

then
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\left(\mu^{\prime\ell},\;\delta_{\lambda^{\prime\ell}(t,x,R)}(\mathrm{d}a)\mu^{\prime\ell}_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}t\right)=\left(\mu^{\prime},\;\Gamma^{\prime}\right)

\mathrm{P}
–a.e. where
\mu^{\prime\ell}_{t}={\cal L}^{\mathrm{P}}(X^{\prime\ell}_{t}|R)
for all
t\in[0,T]
. By using the same argument as in
Proposition

A.2
, we are able to get that
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mathrm{P}\left(\mu^{\prime\ell},{\rm K}(y)(\mathrm{d}i)\mu^{\prime\ell}_{T}(\mathrm{d}y),\mu,R,B\right)^{-1}=\mathrm{P}\left(\mu^{\prime},{\rm K}(y)(\mathrm{d}i)\mu^{\prime}_{T}(\mathrm{d}y),\mu,R,B\right)^{-1}
in
{\cal W}_{p}
for each
{\rm K}\in{\cal K}(\mathbb{R}^{d},{\rm I})
.

∎

We are now ready to show the equivalence result between the canonical formulation used here and the formulation of
Section

2.2
. Let us introduce another set of MFG equilibria. For any contract
\mathfrak{C}=(\phi,\xi,\aleph)
and
\varepsilon\geq 0
, we define

\displaystyle{\cal S}^{\star}(\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon):=\left\{\mathbb{P}\circ(\mu,R)^{-1}:\;\mbox{where }(\alpha,\overline{\mu})\in\mbox{{\rm MFG}}[\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon]\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;R:=\delta_{(\overline{\mu}_{t},\aleph_{t})}(\mathrm{d}m,\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}t\right\}.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
\varepsilon\geq 0
. For any contract
\mathfrak{C}=(\phi,\xi,\aleph)
and probability measure
\mathrm{Q}^{\star}\in{\cal S}^{\star}(\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon)
